Abstract (英文): Multitemporal synthetic aperture radar (SAR) imagery provides continuous, cloud-free observations of crop phenology for agricultural monitoring in cloudy and rainy regions, yet high-accuracy classification typically requires extensive labeled samples. Although self-supervised pre-training mitigates label dependency in remote sensing, existing frameworks primarily target optical imagery or generic spatial SAR scenes, underutilizing temporal backscatter dynamics and polarimetric scattering mechanisms. We propose SAR-TS-BERT, a physics-guided BERT-style self-supervised pre-training framework for SAR time-series crop classification. SAR-TS-BERT integrates backscatter intensities and polarimetric ratio features (VV, VH, and VV/VH) with dual-polarimetric Cloude-Pottier decomposition parameters ($H/α/A$) into a six-band input representation through two core designs: 1) a physics-guided span masking strategy (SpanPhysics) that adaptively selects contiguous masked spans based on temporal backscatter change rates to prioritize key phenological transitions; and 2) a dual-branch cross-modal fusion encoder that models backscatter and polarimetric decomposition features through separate Transformer branches followed by bidirectional cross-attention fusion. On independent test sets in Estonia (EuroCropsML) and the USA (California CDL), SAR-TS-BERT achieves overall accuracies (OAs) of 96.78% and 87.29%, respectively, and attains a mean monthly OA of 99.37% on Chongming Island, Shanghai. On the Estonia benchmark, pre-training improves OA and Macro F1 by 1.17 and 1.59 percentage points over training from scratch under full supervision, and by 2.58 and 5.19 percentage points using only 1% labeled data. Incorporating SAR scattering physics into temporal self-supervised pre-training effectively enhances crop classification accuracy, parcel-level mapping consistency, and few-shot transferability.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Reliable histopathology segmentation requires models that rely on pathology-relevant structures rather than spurious background correlations. In this work, we argue that improving architectures alone is insufficient if models continue to rely on spurious correlations rather than pathology-relevant features. We introduce a causality-inspired evaluation framework based on controlled interventions that explicitly probes model sensitivity to foreground tissue structure and background/domain variation. Building on this, we propose the Causal Reliance Score (CRS), a metric that quantifies the relative sensitivity of model predictions to foreground perturbation versus background/domain variation under controlled interventions. To support this analysis, we design augmentation strategies that act as implicit counterfactual interventions during training, encouraging invariance to nuisance factors while preserving morphology. We demonstrate how these interventions reveal behavioural differences between models that are not captured by standard metrics such as Dice or IoU. While we instantiate our framework using a self-supervised transformer (CAUSE-ViT), the primary contribution of this work is not a new architecture, but a principled intervention-based methodology and evaluation metric for auditing model behaviour in medical image segmentation. Our results suggest that evaluating model behaviour through counterfactual interventions is essential for developing reliable and clinically robust medical image segmentation systems.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Highway work zones are critical edge cases for autonomous vehicles (AVs), combining atypical geometries and jurisdiction-specific regulations that existing benchmarks fail to capture: real-world datasets lack 360° multi-modal, full-scene 3D work zone annotation, and no single recorded site can span the layouts and regulatory variants needed for generalizable models. We present WorkIng, a dual-domain benchmark and training resource. WorkIng-Highway provides a 360° multi-modal work zone benchmark with synchronized 128-channel LiDAR, six RGB cameras, and RTK-GPS across 32 km, yielding 12,000+ annotated frames and 227,399 tracked 3D bounding boxes. WorkIng-CARLA complements this with procedural simulation spanning 24 regulation-aware scenarios across six canonical topologies and six international traffic standards, populated by 100+ custom 3D assets. Benchmarking representative 3D detection and end-to-end planning architectures shows that real-world data remains the primary performance driver, and that scenario-stratified synthetic augmentation improves architectures across 2D recognition, 3D detection, and trajectory planning. Abstract (英文): Multi-head attention in Vision Transformers (ViTs) allows independent attention patterns across heads, but without explicit regularization, heads may learn redundant patterns rather than complementary roles. Correlation-based penalties encourage head disentanglement, yet apply uniform regularization across all training samples. In face recognition (FR), hard samples whose embeddings lie close to competing identities are critical for optimization and generalization. Motivated by this, we propose ViT-HarDis, which conditions attention head disentanglement on sample difficulty: samples with high cosine similarity to negative class centers receive stronger head decorrelation, enforcing specialization where identity ambiguity is highest. Experiments show improvements over both the baseline and uniform disentanglement on most benchmarks. Our analysis shows that ViT-HarDis reduces the similarity of hard samples to competing class centers while decreasing inter-head attention similarity, and that applying disentanglement only to the final transformer layers outperforms regularizing the entire network. Trained on WebFace4M, ViT-HarDis achieves SOTA results among ViT-based methods on IJB-B, IJB-C, and TinyFace Rank-1, while achieving the best IJB-B and TinyFace results when trained on MS1MV3, improving FR without architectural modifications or additional inference cost. Code: https://github.com/TaharChettaoui/Hard-Sample-Conditioned-Attention-for-FR.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Average segmentation accuracy and model count do not reveal whether models fail on the same cases. We present CoFailMed, an audit of correlated failures in pretrained-encoder pools for medical segmentation. Across five 2D tasks, we evaluate 11 public pretrained encoders (a controlled sample, not a census) using frozen features and separately trained $1\times 1$ readouts with four seeds per encoder. Shared-checkpoint pairs have the highest mean per-case Dice correlation. An expanded 17-encoder analysis shows that distinct checkpoints within the same encoder family remain more correlated than cross-family pairs. At the pooled 33rd-percentile Dice threshold, four-member pools drawn from distinct encoders have mean effective sizes of 1.56-2.92, estimated from failure correlations in pools with enough failures and passes per member. In the primary pool, richer readouts and fine-tuning narrow the Dice-correlation gap between pairs sharing a checkpoint (or the DINOv2-B/S release) and all other pairs. Yet dependence between different encoders can increase, and fine-tuning does not consistently increase effective pool size at 33rd-percentile thresholds recomputed for each training recipe. A smaller between-group gap can coexist with greater dependence. Pool evaluation should therefore report absolute dependence and threshold-specific effective size alongside accuracy and nominal pool size. The CoFailMed release provides aligned per-case traces, analysis scripts and study code to reproduce the central audit results.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Vision Transformers represent each image patch through a canonical token produced under one observation condition. That token is a powerful interface, but it is still one realized state of the patch. We introduce PMTT, which establishes two foundations: a patch representation object can extend from one canonical state to a canonical-centered Representation State Family, and identity-preserving variation opens Patch-Level Local Feature Space. We develop four parts of this representation program. Patch Information Acquisition and Dense Orbital Views expose lawful same-patch states. Visible-to-Hidden prediction tests whether observed states reveal structure beyond themselves. Intrinsic analysis identifies low-dimensional, energy-concentrated, patch-specific, predictive geometry. Representation Organization separates acquiring more information from organizing that information, with an analytic Manifold as the central structured representation. We realize PMTT on frozen DINOv3 S/B/L/H+ backbones without neural post-training of the representation. Across two independent 4,096-anchor cohorts, Manifold explains 70.8-79.6% of Hidden variation, compared with 55.0-72.3% for directly Observed states, and wins on more than 95% of anchors at every scale. Effective rank falls from about 2.5 to 2.0 while dominant energy concentration rises with backbone scale. Across six visual capabilities and four scales, Observed exceeds the canonical Center in 21 of 24 conditions, Manifold exceeds Center in 23, and Manifold exceeds Observed in 16. The strongest current consequence appears in physical correspondence, where MegaDepth satisfies Center < Observed < Manifold at every scale. These results show that pretrained visual backbones contain richer patch information than the standard single-token interface directly exposes. Code and experimental assets are available at https://github.com/zixiongwang-ai/PMTT.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Camera localization methods are mostly multi-stage pipelines relying on dedicated and complex maps that are costly to construct, store, and maintain over time. Alternatively, well-structured maps that organize scenes with compact yet expressive primitives are often readily available, providing rich structural and semantic cues that humans can interpret. However, their potential for direct camera localization remains largely unexplored due to the significant modality gap between map primitives and visual observations. This motivates a new concept for localization, which estimates camera poses by grounding query observations to map primitives. In this paper, we instantiate the concept in the context of planar primitives with PGGT, the Primitive-Grounded Geometry Transformer network. Given a single query image and a set of planar map primitives, the network is trained to predict multiple localization targets for feed-forward 6-DoF camera localization that generalizes to unseen environments. With this straightforward and scalable architecture, our method pushes the state-of-the-art localization performance on both dense surface maps and minimal layout models. The code and models are available at https://github.com/3dv-casia/PGGT-Loc

</details>

Abstract (英文): Accurate BEV map prediction is a critical component of autonomous driving systems, enabling downstream planning and control. Recent approaches such as Mask2Map achieve strong performance by predicting vectorized, instance-level map elements directly from multi-view images. However, existing BEV map prediction models operate largely independently of object-level scene understanding, despite the fact that traffic participants frequently occlude or implicitly encode information about road geometry. In this work, we propose a multitask architecture that jointly predicts BEV maps and 3D objects, explicitly enabling information exchange between the two tasks. Building upon Mask2Map, we introduce an object detection branch and incorporate cross-task feature fusion at both the BEV feature level and the instance-query level via deformable attention and cross-attention mechanisms. This design allows map instances to leverage object context while remaining robust to occlusions caused by traffic participants. Experimental results on the nuScenes and Argoverse 2 datasets demonstrate that our approach consistently improves BEV map prediction accuracy, with particularly significant gains of 21% mAP in scenes containing heavy object occlusion.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) are increasingly capable of understanding images and performing complex visual reasoning, suggesting the possibility of using them to automate biological image analysis. This need is becoming increasingly urgent as advances in technology continue to generate ever larger and more diverse microscopy datasets, already exceeding the scale at which manual visual analysis is feasible. With the final aim to use MLLMs for visual comparison of complex phenotypes, we investigate a very simple baseline: comparison of the size of body parts in synthetic images of ants. We find that state-of-the-art open-weight MLLMs fail for some body parts, but not for others, even though the comparison itself is visually trivial for all of them. We evaluate different in-context learning strategies and analyze performance on the related object recognition task. Our results suggest a recognition-driven grounding failure: structures that the models cannot recognize are instead associated with a recognizable semantic concept, impairing the grounding and comparison of both. Further analysis of the vision encoder is consistent with the hypothesis that fine-grained visual structure may be less well preserved without semantic support. Together, our results reveal a surprising limitation of current MLLMs: semantic recognition can gate simple visual comparisons that do not intrinsically require semantic understanding. Our code and data are available at https://github.com/kreshuklab/Open-Weight-MLLMs-Cannot-Compare-What-They-Cannot-Name

</details>

Abstract (英文): High accuracy on streaming video benchmarks does not establish whether a model uses visual history. Our review of OVO-Bench and StreamingBench finds that recent frames suffice for 32% of sampled historical questions, while models also answer many questions correctly from the question and options alone. We introduce TwinPast to test whether answers correctly follow changes in history, using 350 human-reviewed video pairs spanning five historical tasks. Each pair holds the recent video context, question, and ordered options fixed while changing critical historical evidence and the correct answer. Each video is evaluated independently, and PairAcc requires correct answers for both histories. We evaluate 34 model and input configurations. For Qwen3-VL-8B and DeepSeek-Flash, historical inputs improve performance on TwinPast, reversing the recent-frame advantage on existing benchmarks. The strongest evaluated streaming system reaches 49.14% PairAcc, compared with 66.57-91.43% for the evaluated general-purpose models. This gap persists across all five tasks. Case studies further show that models select states from the wrong time or contradict recorded event order despite retaining the relevant evidence. These findings highlight the need to connect historical memory and retrieval with reasoning that uses evidence according to the question's temporal references and event relations.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Human vision is foveated and active: resolution is highest at the fovea and falls off towards the periphery, and fixations are actively directed to locations of interest. Building a machine system that processes information in the same way lets us study gaze behavior in a controlled setting where the system's components and objectives are known. The emergence of gaze characteristics can therefore be examined systematically, which may in turn inform our understanding of biological vision. This requires a model that can sample like the fovea, handle various tasks from classification to dense prediction, and be trained concurrently with a fixation policy. In our study, we present such a system and study its capabilities and the fixation policies that emerge from it. Our system, FoAViS, samples visual input by adopting the established log-polar foveation and accumulates information in a 2D spatial memory. Due to its 2D structure, the same memory can be read out globally for classification or densely for semantic segmentation, without any architectural change. Fixations are drawn from a 2D map by a policy trained concurrently with the task. We find that learned fixation policies select more informative, task-specific locations than non-learned heuristics. These policies also shape the task representations in a beneficial way: for both classification and segmentation, models trained concurrently with a policy outperform models trained with random locations, even when the latter are provided with learned scanpaths. Moreover, these scanpaths exhibit similarities to human gaze behavior by capturing aspects of empirical scanpath characteristics. Our work marks an important step away from standard passive, single-pass architectures, towards biologically grounded models of vision. It opens a path for studying goal-directed gaze behavior and information representation in the visual system.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Modern visual tokenizers aim to preserve fine-grained visual details while learning high-level semantic representations for downstream generation and understanding. Query-based 1D visual tokenizers encode images into flexible latent sequences, yet how individual learnable queries gather and encode image information remains poorly understood. We reveal that these queries spontaneously develop complementary roles under semantic supervision: a small set of Content queries captures high-level semantic information, while a larger set of Detail queries preserves fine-grained visual details for reconstruction. To adapt token processing to these heterogeneous roles, we introduce MoETok, a 1D visual tokenizer that routes tokens through sparsely activated experts. Jointly optimized with reconstruction and semantic objectives, the router learns role-specialized processing paths adapted to each token's representation. Across model scales and token budgets, MoETok consistently improves reconstruction, semantic representation, and autoregressive generation over dense tokenizers under matched supervision and active computation. On ImageNet, it combines strong reconstruction and semantic representation with state-of-the-art class-conditional generation within the 1D tokenizer family. We further validate MoETok at scale in a unified autoregressive model jointly trained for understanding and generation with a next-token cross-entropy objective. The resulting model combines competitive visual understanding with state-of-the-art generation among unified discrete models, matching or surpassing diffusion-based models such as BAGEL and Qwen-Image on benchmarks including DPG-Bench (87.61), GenEval2 (40.04 GM), and WISE (0.49).

</details>

Abstract (英文): Video generation models synthesize realistic motion, but can they respond correctly to changes in initial geometry? We introduce GeoFlip, a video generation benchmark centered on physical outcome flips induced by geometry. With shared physical settings and textual prompts held fixed, GeoFlip applies controlled interventions to support extent, object placement, and mechanism configuration, constructing paired scenes with contrasting reference events, such as remaining stable versus falling or tipping. These pairs test whether models translate geometric differences in the first frame into correct physical evolution. The benchmark comprises 482 scene pairs and 964 reference videos across six physical categories, with reference outcomes established through procedural scene construction, physics simulation, and numerical validation and verification of physical events. We evaluate 16 models for image to video generation along three dimensions: event correctness, structural integrity, and similarity to reference videos. We examine evaluation consistency with independent vision language judges. On the 442 conditions shared by all models, the highest event correctness averaged across tasks is 27.98%, revealing substantial limitations in physical generation conditioned on geometry. Model strengths vary across physical mechanisms, and the three evaluation dimensions capture distinct capabilities. Typical failures include ignoring geometric changes, generating incorrect object motion, bending rigid objects, and introducing additional components. Rank correlations between judges reach 0.906 for event correctness and 0.929 for structural integrity. By combining controlled geometric interventions, verifiable physical references, and multidimensional evaluation, GeoFlip assesses progress from visual realism toward physical consistency conditioned on geometry.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Autoregressive video generation enables efficient inference through streaming prediction. However, existing methods are typically restricted to fixed sampling steps, limiting the flexibility to balance generation quality and efficiency, while one-step generation suffers from degraded visual quality and error accumulation over long sequences, leading to content drift and generation collapse. To address these issues, we propose Any Forcing, a unified framework for achieving Any-Step Generality and One-Step Stability. Specifically, we introduce a unified adversarial discriminator that jointly performs real-distribution alignment, inter-step consistency alignment, and first-frame quality enhancement, enabling flexible sampling while preserving one-step generation quality. Furthermore, we propose a training-free adaptive long-term memory selection mechanism that selects historical states based on global semantic relevance and local value contribution, while remapping their temporal positions to preserve temporal relationships within the memory cache, thereby enabling stable one-step long-video generation. Experiments demonstrate that Any Forcing achieves high-quality and stable one-step autoregressive video generation, achieving 20.9 FPS and 24.7 FPS for short and long video generation on a single H100 GPU, respectively.

</details>

Abstract (英文): We study how to learn a generative model of clean images using only noisy observations. Diffusion processes and their generalizations to non-Gaussian noise require posterior predictions at every noise level. We first show that it is possible to learn these predictions from independent noisy views using Noise2Noise regression, but only at or above the noise level of the training data. To learn the predictions below that level, needed to generate clean images, we impose consistency: a prediction should equal its expectation after continuing the sampling process. We derive conditions under which consistency uniquely determines the continuation to the clean image distribution and verify them for Poisson, binomial, Gamma, and negative-binomial measurements. Experiments on CIFAR-10, photon-counting microscopy, and synthetic aperture radar show that the required posterior predictions can be learned from noisy views alone and that the consistency relation repairs the continuation beyond the noise level of the data, which severely deteriorates without consistency.

</details>

Abstract (英文): A panoramic video captures all directions around the camera, but relevant evidence can appear in different directions and at different times. Understanding it requires models to connect observations across the viewing sphere and time. We introduce PanoVideoBench, the first manually annotated benchmark for comprehensive panoramic video understanding. Its 1,000 questions span five categories and 15 question types, with diagnostic annotations for target search and panoramic capabilities. Evaluation of 11 proprietary, open-source, and panoramic-specialized models identifies cross-view tracking and sphere-time evidence integration as their weakest capabilities. To strengthen these capabilities, we construct PanoVideoCoT, the first reasoning dataset grounded in real panoramic video, through sphere-time-grounded synthesis that converts verified panoramic observations and successful agentic explorations into 13,465 QA-CoT pairs. Starting from a general video MLLM, PanoVideoReasoner learns from this corpus through Panoramic CoT SFT and Evidence-Privileged On-Policy Self-Distillation, a panoramic instantiation of Vision-OPD's OPSD in which the teacher uses verified perspective views to guide student-generated trajectories. It reaches 55.7% on PanoVideoBench, improving the backbone by 12.5 points and the previous SOTA by 2.6. The gains concentrate on the same spatial, cross-view, and temporal capabilities exposed by the benchmark, showing that grounded sphere-time supervision can equip a general video MLLM with panoramic reasoning.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Self-supervised learning relies on asymmetry between what a model observes and what it must explain. Recent methods learn 3D geometry from unposed images by predicting cameras and scene structure, then rendering held-out views. To avoid trivial solutions, they impose this asymmetry in the input space, restricting how views interact through separate branches or masked attention. We revisit this design with geometric accuracy, rather than rendering quality, as the main objective. When a held-out view lies between context views, the context already constrains the geometry it observes. We therefore impose the asymmetry in the 3D representation space, excluding held-out views only from the reconstructed scene while a shared backbone uses all views for correspondence. This improves depth and pose estimation over input-space designs. We further exploit view density: sparse and dense observations depict the same scene but provide different amounts of correspondence. Aligning sparse-context features with dense-context features supplies a geometric training signal alongside rendering, without a pretrained geometric teacher. We additionally align features with a frozen vision foundation model, which brings further gains. None of these signals requires geometric annotation, and the resulting model improves geometry estimation over prior self-supervised methods. Controlled experiments isolate the contribution of each component, and self-supervised pretraining also improves subsequent supervised training.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Distribution Matching Distillation (DMD) trains a few-step student using the difference between a teacher's score and an online critic's estimate of the student distribution's score. We investigate what the critic needs, in capacity and in information, to provide a reliable training signal. On the capacity side, controlled experiments across seven SiT model sizes on ImageNet show that increasing teacher size while keeping a small critic fixed can degrade student quality and cause training collapse. Scaling the critic alongside the teacher, a strategy we call joint scaling, prevents collapse in our experiments and allows the student to benefit from larger teachers. On the information side, rather than forcing the critic to model a step-agnostic mixture of student outputs, we introduce a step-aware critic (STAC) that conditions on the student's generation step to estimate step-specific scores. Together, joint scaling and STAC consistently improve few-step distillation across class-conditional ImageNet generation, text-to-image generation with SDXL and FLUX.2, and text-to-video generation with Wan2.1.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Dense 3D tracking from monocular video requires establishing point correspondences over time, estimating their depth, and inferring their trajectories during occlusion. Existing methods typically learn these tasks jointly, a demanding objective given the scarcity of 4D supervision. We show that strong pretrained 2D trackers and 3D geometry models provide an effective foundation: lifted trajectories, with occluded segments simply interpolated, already rival dedicated 3D trackers using the same geometry. However, lifted trajectories inherit correspondence and depth errors, and under occlusion, their depth comes from the occluder rather than the tracked point. We introduce ReLift, an efficient feed-forward framework that recasts dense 3D tracking as geometric trajectory repair and completion. At its core, a trajectory corrector alternates attention along and across trajectories to preserve reliable observations, repair corrupted positions, and complete missing motion. Using only geometric inputs allows us to train directly on 3D trajectories with simulated lifting errors, decoupling training from images and from any specific tracker or geometry model. Concentrating 4D supervision on trajectory refinement yields a reusable model that consistently improves lifted trajectories across trackers and geometry providers without retraining, so it benefits directly from future advances in either component. On WorldTrack, ReLift achieves state-of-the-art mean accuracy, improving on the strongest baselines by 2.1 APD on all points and 3.0 on dynamic points, with faster configurations remaining competitive with leading 3D trackers while running faster than 4RC. Visit our supplementry page for more examples and code.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Unified Multimodal Models (UMMs) achieve remarkable success on diverse multimodal understanding and generation tasks, yet they still struggle to effectively leverage language-based chain-of-thought reasoning for image generation. As a result, existing reasoning-augmented UMMs often fail to inherit the test-time scaling and fine-grained controllability observed in large language models. We attribute this failure to a fundamental modality mismatch: reasoning is performed in a discrete token space, whereas image generation operates in a continuous space. To bridge this gap, we introduce a Latent Reasoning framework in Continuous space (LARC), which alleviates the token-level bottleneck by reasoning over continuous hidden states. Specifically, the training framework consists of two stages: (i) curriculum supervised fine-tuning (SFT), and (ii) information-gain reinforcement learning (RL). First, the curriculum SFT stage gradually converts token-level reasoning into latent reasoning. Subsequently, the information-gain RL stage self-evolves latent reasoning without ground truth supervision. Experiments across diverse text-to-image generation benchmarks show that LARC consistently improves generation quality, prompt following, and compositional fidelity. Notably, LARC achieves state-of-the-art performance on GenEval with a 7%p gain over BAGEL and demonstrates clear test-time scaling behavior, outperforming token-based generation under both parallel and sequential test-time scaling strategies.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Radiologists' visual attention can supervise medical image learning, but how reader panel composition affects its value remains untested. We evaluated GazeRIB (Gaze-guided Radiologist-Informed Blackout), which masks unviewed regions during training and uses unmasked images at inference. Eight experts repeatedly read the same 80 chest X-rays (CXRs). We compared individual readers with three-, five- and eight-reader panels across seven architectures and 10 seeds, evaluating 910 models on a separate 2,897-CXR cohort for four-class severity prediction. Every panel improved macro F1 over the no-gaze baseline by 5.33-7.47 percentage points (pp; Bonferroni-adjusted p < 0.007); no individual reader reached significance on this endpoint. Three readers captured 73-80% of the eight-reader gain in macro F1, the area under the receiver-operating-characteristic curve (ROC-AUC) and the area under the precision-recall curve (AU-PRC). On external validation without retraining, all four panels improved ROC-AUC on SIIM-FISABIO-RSNA by +1.93 to +4.24 pp (p <= 0.004); on MIMIC-Eye no panel differed significantly from baseline, whereas the single-reader average declined (-1.40 pp, p = 0.008). Reader composition therefore influenced performance, although transfer depended on the cohort.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Hyperspectral tree species classification is challenging due to limited and imbalanced class labels, spectral mixing (overlapping light signatures from multiple species), and ecological heterogeneity (variability among ecological systems). Addressing these challenges requires methods that integrate biological and structural characteristics of vegetation, such as canopy architecture and interspecific interactions, rather than relying solely on spectral signatures. This paper presents a biologically informed, semi-supervised deep learning method that integrates multi-sensor Earth observation data, specifically hyperspectral imaging (HSI) and airborne laser scanning (ALS), with expert, ecological knowledge. The approach relies on biologically inspired pseudo-labelling over a precomputed canopy graph, yielding improved classification with a lightweight classifier. In addition, ecological priors are constructed using an LLM-assisted, citation-constrained workflow and encoded as a cohabitation matrix with likelihoods of species occurring together. These priors are incorporated into the pseudo-labelling strategy, effectively introducing expert knowledge into the model. Experiments on two real-world forest datasets showed macro-F1 improvements of 6.01 and 4.85 percentage points over the strongest classical HSI--ALS reference pipelines.

</details>

Abstract (英文): The proliferation of remote sensing platforms has generated an unprecedented diversity of Earth observation data, creating both the opportunity and the challenge of extracting transferable knowledge across sensors, scales, and environments. Foundation models have emerged as a promising framework for this challenge, leveraging large datasets to learn representations that generalize across tasks and sensing conditions in real-world Earth observation. However, many current approaches rely on very large pretraining datasets and remain constrained by fixed sensor configurations, limited observation-type flexibility, or narrow evaluation settings. These limitations are particularly relevant for ecological and environmental applications, where data are often heterogeneous across platforms, resolutions, and data sources. We introduce FLORO, a multimodal geospatial foundation model designed to learn transferable representations from a comparatively small, heterogeneous remote-sensing pretraining dataset. FLORO is pretrained using masked autoencoding on a heterogeneous combination of Sentinel-1, Sentinel-2, SkySAT imagery, terrain data, and UAV-derived products. To accommodate real-world sensor variability, FLORO incorporates availability-aware inputs that indicate which spectral bands and auxiliary observations are present in each sample, enabling a unified input space across heterogeneous sensor configurations. We evaluated FLORO on the PANGAEA benchmark under a frozen-encoder protocol across scene classification, segmentation, and regression tasks. Despite being pretrained on a smaller dataset than competing foundation models, FLORO achieved strong and stable transfer across optical, optical-SAR, and optical-elevation benchmarks spanning medium-resolution satellite, airborne, and ultra-high-resolution UAV imagery. FLORO obtained the second-best average segmentation performance across six PANGAEA benchmarks, the third-best average rank across three scene-classification benchmarks, and excelled in regression tasks, while qualitative results showed improved preservation of spatial structure in flood, urban, biomass, and canopy-height prediction settings. Overall, these results position FLORO as a practical framework for ecological and environmental monitoring under heterogeneous sensing conditions. To support benchmarking and reproducibility, the source code and PANGAEA evaluation integration, including access to the benchmark datasets, are available at https://github.com/jorlrodriguezg/floro , with pretrained model checkpoints at https://huggingface.co/jorlrodriguezg/floro .

</details>

Abstract (英文): High-definition (HD) vector maps are essential to trajectory forecasting, yet existing methods often use them only as passive context and remain dependent on identity-consistent tracking histories. We propose a unified geometry-centric forecasting framework that treats road-space geometry as an active computational substrate for both future prediction and history recovery under tracking-available and no-tracking conditions. The framework uses a common road-space representation and topology-guided decoupled decoder architecture across both settings, while differing only in how motion history is constructed. With reliable tracking, agent history is incrementally aggregated across shifting local frames for efficient streaming inference. Without tracking, frame-wise detections are projected into lane-space occupancy memories, from which target-specific history is recovered through geometry-conditioned querying. The decoder then generates topology-consistent motion intents and realizes them as final trajectories using surrounding-agent interactions. Experiments on Argoverse 2 (AV2), Argoverse 1 (AV1), and the Waymo Open Motion Dataset (WOMD) show that the proposed framework achieves competitive performance under reliable tracking and supports low-latency streaming inference, reaching 44.32 ms per frame with pre-encoded maps. Under no-tracking conditions, the occupancy-based pathway preserves most of the tracking-available performance on the primary K = 6 metrics, with relative increases in minFDE 6 , minADE 6 , and MR 6 limited to 6%–8% on AV2 and below 3% on AV1 and WOMD. Additional validation on real sensor data with annotated detections and a tracking-by-detection association pipeline shows that the occupancy-based pathway remains robust to tracking identity errors, whereas identity-dependent methods degrade substantially. These results demonstrate that actively exploiting road-space geometry can substantially reduce trajectory forecasting’s reliance on upstream tracking.

</details>

Abstract (英文): With the rapid shrinkage of Arctic sea ice, predicting its spatio-temporal changes has become an important technical basis for studies of polar climate. Existing physics-driven and data-driven paradigms show respective advantages in representing sea-ice process mechanisms and learning complex spatio-temporal patterns; however, both remain challenged in balancing prediction accuracy with physics consistency. Therefore, this study proposes a daily-scale spatio-temporal sea ice thickness (SIT) prediction method driven by both data and physics, namely the Physics-Constrained Generative Adversarial Network (PCGAN). To incorporate sea-ice physics principles into the data-driven prediction process, PCGAN is designed at three levels: the generator, the discriminators, and physics-rule constraints. First, a Physics-Augmented Cross-Process Interaction (PA-CPI) module is designed in the generator to enhance the ability of the model to represent the physically coupled mechanisms among multiple geophysics variables, including sea ice, snow, wind fields, and temperature. Second, a spatio-temporal dual-discriminator architecture is proposed to jointly constrain the local spatial structures and temporal increments of SIT. Finally, the freezing-degree-day growth relationship and the daily-scale thickness change-rate relationship are transformed into bounded physics consistency scores and embedded into temporal adversarial learning to improve the physics plausibility of the generator-predicted results. Experimental results in the Arctic Ocean during the cold season show that PCGAN maintains stable performance under different random initializations. Its average RMSE is 0.1035 m, which is 12.73% lower than that of MSS-STFormer, the best-performing baseline model. Physics-related statistical metrics, such as the anomalous thinning rate, also show that the predictions of PCGAN are closer to the observed statistical characteristics than those of the other deep learning models. Multidimensional spatio-temporal analyses further confirm the stable predictive performance of PCGAN under different prediction conditions. The interpretability analysis shows that the model’s responses to multiple geophysical factors are broadly consistent with established physical understanding of sea-ice evolution. Ablation experiments and threshold-sensitivity analyses verify the effectiveness of the key model structures and the physics consistency constraints. PCGAN demonstrates the potential of incorporating physics consistency constraints into data-driven frameworks to improve SIT prediction accuracy and physical plausibility, providing methodological support for Arctic SIT prediction during the cold season.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Net primary productivity (NPP) plays a central role in biological carbon pump (BCP) and global carbon cycling, but NPP estimates in high-latitude regions remain highly uncertain despite their disproportional contribution to the global carbon sink. Previous studies identified discrepancies among satellite- and in situ-derived NPP estimates, but the drivers of disagreement remain poorly resolved due to limited high-frequency observations. Here, we present continuous depth-resolved estimates of in situ NPP from the Central Labrador Sea (56°N) in the subpolar Northwest Atlantic, reconstructed from daily bio-optical profiles collected between May and November 2016, and a 40-year dataset of photosynthesis parameters derived from 14C photosynthesis-irradiance (P-I) incubation experiments. Comparing with two satellite-based models showed satellite models overestimated NPP by factors of 2.5–4 from the study period. Statistical analyses identified the P-I parameterization as the most influential contributor to the disagreement between satellite and in situ NPP estimates. Given the importance of NPP to estimates of the biological carbon pump, our findings highlight the need for regionally appropriate P-I parameterization in NPP models, particularly in high-latitude regions.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Live fuel moisture content (LFMC) influences vegetation flammability, but field measurements are too sparse to describe its variation over large areas. We present, to our knowledge, the first LFMC framework that combines observations from multiple optical satellite sensors within the same trained models. More than two decades of GlobeLFMC 2.0 measurements are matched to Terra and Aqua Moderate Resolution Imaging Spectroradiometer (MODIS), Visible Infrared Imaging Radiometer Suite (VIIRS), Landsat 8/9, Sentinel-2 and Sentinel-3. We screen the vegetation surrounding sampling locations and adjust reflectance for differences between sensors before calculating vegetation indices. Weather, topography and time of year provide additional information. Separate models are trained for grasses, shrubs and trees. We test prediction on dates not used for training at sites where other dates contribute to training. At each site, records using the same satellite image are kept in either the training or the test set. The final Random Forest models achieve R2 values of 0.715, 0.693 and 0.691, respectively. Models trained on the combined archive give higher R2 and lower root mean square error than models trained on one satellite product in all 18 product–vegetation comparisons on identical test observations. Paired 95% confidence intervals support the improvement in 16 comparisons. Testing at sites excluded from training gives lower accuracy. The method produces LFMC maps at the selected satellite product’s resolution, from 20–30 m to 300–500 m.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Mediterranean grasslands exhibit strong seasonality, complicating comparisons between satellite-derived vegetation greenness and cumulative biomass production. This study examined how temporal integration and segmentation influence the association between the MODIS Normalized Difference Vegetation Index (NDVI) and cumulative biomass production simulated by the SIMPAST ecohydrological model. MODIS NDVI time series (MOD09Q1; eight-day composites; 250 m) spanning 2000–2025 were analysed at three grassland sites in central Spain. Non-accumulated NDVI and two cumulative metrics, based on simple and conditional accumulation, were compared with simulated production under conventional seasons and five fixed eco-phenological periods. Simulations used six original meteorological forcing trajectories and an additional forcing dataset based on observed weather. The temporally matched reference relationship between non-accumulated NDVI and simulated eight-day biomass production yielded mean R2 values of 0.188, 0.069, and 0.073 at Piñuecar, Colmenar Viejo, and Tielmes, respectively. Pooled relationships between cumulative NDVI and cumulative simulated biomass were substantially stronger (R2 = 0.714–0.746 for simple accumulation and 0.600–0.723 for conditional accumulation), although these higher values partly reflected their shared within-year temporal progression. Observed meteorological forcing showed the same general pattern, with interval-level reference R2 values of 0.122–0.263 and substantially stronger pooled relationships for the cumulative metrics. However, first differencing reduced mean R2 for simple accumulation to 0.180 at Piñuecar, 0.004 at Colmenar Viejo, and 0.097 at Tielmes, indicating that the strong cumulative associations depended partly on shared within-year progression. Conventional seasons generally yielded the strongest associations in autumn or spring, whereas eco-phenological segmentation generally concentrated them in winter-to-early spring growth. Neither eco-phenological segmentation nor conditional accumulation consistently increased R2 relative to its alternative. These findings distinguish correspondence between cumulative seasonal trajectories from associations at individual observation intervals. Temporal segmentation provides a complementary interpretative framework, but high cumulative R2 values do not establish predictive performance. Operational applications require independent field evaluation of whether these indicators capture biomass production variability beyond shared seasonal progression.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Accurate precipitation estimation is crucial for water management and flood reconstructions, yet remains challenging due to sparse ground observations and systematic satellite biases. Despite the rise of deep learning in multi-source fusion, controlled comparisons under a unified framework remain scarce, and the roles of window size and precipitation product combinations remain underexplored. This study evaluated five deep learning architectures, namely 1D CNN, 2D CNN, 1D–2D CNN, 3D CNN, and Transformer, for fusing three precipitation products (IMERG, MSWEP, and ERA5-Land) to estimate station-scale daily precipitation in the Yellow River Basin, China. Results showed that deep learning models consistently outperformed individual products, weighting methods, and machine learning methods, with Transformer achieving the best overall performance, followed by 3D CNN; MSWEP led the standalone products. Spatial improvements were more pronounced in densely gauged areas, but Transformer also performed well in the sparse western regions. All products and fusion models tended to overestimate no-rain and light-rain events and underestimate moderate-to-heavy, and extreme events, with Transformer yielding the largest reductions in conditional RMSE and BIAS for heavy and extreme events. Enlarging the spatial or temporal window individually improved performance, and combining both worked even better. Ablation experiments revealed that IMERG contributed little unique information, whereas incorporating more products generally improved Transformer performance. Overall, Transformer demonstrated superior performance in fusing precipitation products and enhancing estimation accuracy.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Wavelength-conditioned remote sensing foundation models accept flexible spectral configurations, but compatibility does not ensure effective spectral utilization. We investigate this issue for hyperspectral remote sensing salient object detection and propose SpecAudit, an offline framework diagnosing input discriminability, entry-kernel geometry, and layer-wise spectral readability, together with Two-Level Spectral Adaptation (TLSA). On the 32-band hyperspectral remote sensing salient object detection dataset (HRSSD), expanding a Dynamic One-For-All (DOFA)-based pipeline from 3 to 32 bands yields no stable saliency gain. A ground-truth-assisted image-specific diagnostic readout, fitted and evaluated on spatially separated regions of each test image, yields ΔSα=+0.0418, indicating substantial image-specific spectral discriminability despite the absence of a stable direct prediction gain. TLSA combines task-shared Band Amplitude Calibration, using one learnable gain per band before the frozen entry, with Self-Guided Spectral Refinement, which fits an image-specific spectral discriminant from the coarse prediction at inference. Across five training seeds and paired image-level tests, both adaptation levels provide positive incremental improvements. TLSA increases mean Sα from 0.6472 to 0.6613, yields a positive paired improvement over the three-band reference, and exceeds the highest mean Sα among nine retrained published methods by 3.5%. These results indicate that hyperspectral gains depend on how spectral evidence is exposed and used, not only on available band count. The complete TLSA implementation and SpecAudit diagnostic code will be publicly released upon publication.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Hyperspectral image classification is an important research topic in the field of remote sensing. Its core objective is to accurately identify different land-cover categories by jointly exploiting the spatial information and spectral characteristics of pixels. However, the complex and diverse spatial structures of ground objects in hyperspectral images make it challenging for existing methods to fully capture intricate spatial patterns. To address this issue, this paper proposes a dual-branch spatial–spectral transformer with spatial feature enhancement under a spatial–spectral joint representation learning framework. Specifically, in the spatial branch, a deformable spatial feature fusion module is introduced, which combines grouped deformable convolution with Transformer to achieve fine-grained modeling of irregular spatial structures. Subsequently, a global spatial semantic aggregation module is designed to further integrate spatial features and enhance their discriminative capability. In the spectral branch, Transformer is employed to model long-range spectral dependencies, while a convolutional feed-forward network is introduced to strengthen the representation ability of critical spectral information. Experimental results demonstrate that the proposed method achieves competitive classification performance on four benchmark datasets.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Accurate forest aboveground biomass (AGB) estimates are needed for forest inventories and carbon assessment, yet optical, synthetic aperture radar (SAR), and spaceborne lidar products describe forest properties at different spatial supports. We present LAPFuse, a multimodal framework that combines dense Sentinel-2 and Sentinel-1 observations with sparse GEDI Level 4A footprint-level aboveground biomass density records. Modality-specific encoders preserve the sensing characteristics of each input. Fixed language anchors regularize their projected representations, while distance-weighted sparse–dense attention relates GEDI footprint tokens to spatially distributed Sentinel features. A physics-informed neural process then produces a predictive mean and interval using an allometry-based soft constraint. We evaluate LAPFuse with 1847 permanent plots from the 2022–2024 growing seasons in three forest regions of China. On the held-out set of 369 plots, LAPFuse achieves R2=0.882, RMSE =15.34 Mg ha−1, and MAE =9.87 Mg ha−1. Its 95% prediction intervals attain a coverage probability of 0.949 with a mean width of 67.2 Mg ha−1. Sequential ablation shows progressive improvements as the alignment, attention, probabilistic, and allometric components are introduced. Zero-shot cross-region R2 ranges from 0.762 to 0.798, and adaptation with 100 target-region plots reduces the performance gap to 0.1–1.5% relative to in-domain performance. These results demonstrate the value of support-aware multimodal fusion for plot-level AGB estimation under this sensor configuration and these regional conditions.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Ship detection in range-compressed (RC) synthetic aperture radar (SAR) images requires recognizing spatially extended responses rather than compact target appearances. Because elongated responses are not unique to ships, clutter can interfere with both feature aggregation and candidate selection. We propose FM-DETR, a frequency–morphology collaborative Transformer that extends RC-aware feature modeling to decoder-query initialization. An asymmetric cross-scale feature pyramid uses orthogonal strip convolutions to accommodate the unequal range and azimuth extents of target responses. A Fourier-gated intra-scale interaction module complements spatial self-attention with learnable spectral modulation, while a content-dependent gate regulates the spectral residual. The refined representation provides spatial guidance for multiscale candidate scoring and query selection. Evaluation follows the official RCShip-1.0 split, with synthetic training samples and scene-derived test samples reconstructed in the RC domain. FM-DETR achieves average precision (AP@0.5) of 90.14%, a precision of 89.53%, and a recall of 84.42%. With the backbone and training settings held fixed, the complete model improves AP@0.5 by 4.00 percentage points over the baseline. These results support integrating direction-sensitive aggregation, spectral refinement, and guided query initialization for ship detection in RC images.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Forest canopy height (FCH) is a key parameter for estimating forest aboveground biomass (AGB) and carbon storage. The integration of GEDI relative height products and single-angle imagery has been widely applied in FCH estimation. However, GEDI geolocation errors and the canopy underestimation of traditional single-angle imagery in dense forests limit FCH estimation accuracy. To mitigate these limitations, this study proposes an FCH estimation method that integrates GaoFen-7 (GF-7) stereo pairs, GEDI ground elevation, and Terrestrial Ecosystem Carbon Inventory Satellite (TECIS) multi-angle data. First, this study integrated GF-7 stereo pairs and GEDI elevation to generate a discrete Canopy Height Model (CHM) dataset, providing reliable training samples for FCH estimation. Secondly, to mitigate the canopy underestimation issues associated with traditional single-angle features in FCH retrieval, we further introduced TECIS multi-angle data. To fully exploit its potential for structural characterization, we combined TECIS multi-angle features and topographic features with the discrete CHM dataset, utilizing a Bayesian-optimized LightGBM model to estimate FCH. The results show that: (1) The discrete CHM dataset constructed in this study (R2 = 0.65, RMSE = 2.39 m) outperformed the GEDI RH95 product without geolocation correction (R2 = 0.37, RMSE = 3.24 m). (2) After incorporating TECIS multi-angle features into the FCH inversion model, the predictive accuracy was improved, with the coefficient of determination R2 increasing from 0.36 to 0.51, and the RMSE and MAE decreasing by 0.33 m and 0.31 m. Meanwhile, the mean bias (MB) of trees above 15 m decreased from −2.12 m to −1.49 m, and the relative bias (rBias) decreased from −12.41% to −8.78%. (3) Independent validations of the estimation results using ALS-derived CHM (R2 = 0.46, RMSE = 2.40 m) and field measurements (R2 = 0.50, RMSE = 2.95 m) confirmed the overall predictive capability of the model.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Remote sensing provides an effective means of estimating forest aboveground biomass (AGB) over large areas. However, empirical models calibrated in a specific region may lose predictive accuracy when transferred elsewhere because predictor–AGB relationships can vary with forest structure and environmental conditions. In addition, the complex nonlinear relationships learned by many machine learning models are not explicitly linked to ecological processes, limiting their ecological interpretability. To address these challenges, we developed a height-based allometric framework that incorporates the kernel normalized difference vegetation index (kNDVI) derived from Sentinel-2 imagery and leaf area index (LAI) indirectly estimated from UAV-LiDAR point clouds into the scaling relationship between mean stand height and AGB. By allowing the scaling exponent to vary with canopy spectral and structural conditions, the proposed framework was designed to account for spatial variation in the height–biomass relationship while retaining an explicit allometric structure. Its performance was compared with four commonly used machine learning methods: k-nearest neighbors (kNN), random forest (RF), extreme gradient boosting (XGBoost), and Bayesian Regularized Neural Network (BRNN). Within the calibration region, the proposed allometric model achieved an R2 of 0.67, an RMSE of 17.74 Mg ha−1, and an rRMSE of 15.17%, closely approaching the performance of RF, which was the most accurate machine learning model (R2 = 0.69, RMSE = 17.20 Mg ha−1, and rRMSE = 14.71%). More pronounced differences emerged when the models were evaluated using independent samples located outside the calibration area. Under this spatial transfer test, the allometric model retained an R2 of 0.48, with an RMSE of 22.31 Mg ha−1 and an rRMSE of 20.83%. In comparison, BRNN was the best-performing machine learning method in the same external validation but achieved an R2 of 0.32, an RMSE of 25.44 Mg ha−1, and an rRMSE of 23.75%. Introducing either LAI or kNDVI into the scaling exponent provided greater explanatory value than using a fixed exponent, while the kNDVI-based formulation (R2 = 0.41, RMSE = 23.60 Mg ha−1, and rRMSE = 22.03%) exhibited stronger spatial transferability than its LAI-based counterpart (R2 = 0.37, RMSE = 24.42 Mg ha−1, and rRMSE = 22.80%). Although all evaluated models experienced accuracy losses outside the calibration region, the reduction was substantially greater for the machine learning approaches. The proposed framework therefore provides predictive accuracy comparable to that of machine learning models within the calibration area while offering greater ecological interpretability and stronger performance under spatial transfer. This approach provides a basis for large-area forest AGB estimation by integrating optical observations with canopy structure information and could potentially be extended to applications combining optical imagery with structural information derived from satellite LiDAR. Future research could incorporate spatial autocorrelation and spatial heterogeneity into the proposed framework to further reduce uncertainty in forest AGB estimation.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Accurate wind speed nowcasting is critical for wind-risk assessment, traffic management, and emergency response. However, existing deep learning methods still suffer from inadequate multi-source meteorological data fusion, multi-step error accumulation, and performance decay at high-wind-speed thresholds. To address these issues, this paper proposes STGAF-Net, a multi-source spatiotemporal gated adaptive fusion network. It uses radar reflectivity and surface wind speed as complementary inputs, employs collaborative local–global encoding to capture local details and long-range spatial dependencies, and adaptively integrates multi-source features through stage-wise gating. Temporally, non-recurrent spatiotemporal modeling dynamically adjusts the contribution of each historical time step based on sequence evolution and time intervals, enabling parallel prediction of future wind fields. This end-to-end framework effectively bolsters spatial structure preservation and long-range dependency modeling for severe winds. The experimental results demonstrate that STGAF-Net achieves the strongest overall performance among seven learning-based baselines and a non-learning persistence forecast on two datasets, particularly at high-wind-speed thresholds and at later lead times within the 2-h nowcasting horizon.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Forest above-ground biomass (AGB) is essential for understanding forest carbon stocks and ecosystem dynamics. Despite the strong potential of synthetic aperture radar (SAR) for forest monitoring, retrieving AGB from SAR observations remains difficult because the relationship between SAR scattering responses and forest biomass is highly nonlinear, spatially variable, and often difficult to represent with both accuracy and interpretability. To address this issue, this study proposes PhySR-Bio, a multi-stage physics-aware symbolic regression framework for interpretable SAR-based forest biomass retrieval. The physics-aware design is achieved by incorporating SAR scattering-related variables, incidence angle information, and physically meaningful feature combinations into symbolic regression. Unlike black box regression models, PhySR-Bio derives an analytical formula in which the selected variables and their mathematical relationships can be directly inspected. On the independent test set, PhySR-Bio achieved an RMSE of 45.083 Mg/ha, an MAE of 33.997 Mg/ha, and an R of 0.527. It reduced RMSE and MAE by up to 6.35% and 8.57%, respectively, compared with representative semi-empirical formulas, and achieved a test RMSE within 2.3% of the best-performing black box machine learning model while retaining an explicit and interpretable expression.

Cross-validated temperature compensation improved 1020 nm AOD estimates (RMSE 0.0234 to 0.0199) and precipitable water estimates (0.1177 to 0.1100 cm) but did not improve AOD estimates at 870 nm. Abstract (英文): The quality of the SWOT-derived marine gravity field and its subsequent refinement are investigated in the Western Pacific (120–140°E, 0–30°N). To further refine this field, three tree-based ensemble learning methods—Random Forest (RF), histogram-based gradient boosting (HGB) and LightGBM (LGBM)—are trained on 67 multi-source features with the SWOT-minus-shipborne residual as the target, and validated under two spatially isolated strategies: a 2° × 2° spatial block hold-out with a 10 km buffer and a cruise-wise hold-out over seven complete cruises, in which training samples within 10 km of any test point are removed. All three methods reduce the error. On the block hold-out, LGBM performs best (RMSE 2.398 mGal, a 3.6% improvement over the 2.488 mGal baseline), followed by HGB (2.402 mGal, 3.5%) and RF (2.422 mGal, 2.7%); on the cruise hold-out the gains are markedly larger, with HGB best (RMSE 1.703 mGal, a 8.6% improvement over the 1.863 mGal baseline), then LGBM (1.714 mGal, 8.0%) and RF (1.794 mGal, 3.7%). A consistent reversal is found between the two assessment levels: RF leads on the in-training out-of-fold RMSE but is weakest on unseen cruises, indicating that RF is competitive within the training distribution yet weaker than boosting methods when extrapolating to unsurveyed areas. Power spectral density analysis shows that the improvements concentrate at wavelengths of 8–45 km, whereas wavenumber below 0.0221 km−1 are slightly degraded.

Abstract (英文): Dense fixed-wing UAV swarms can produce overlapping HRRPs, for which echo separation may truncate extended-target responses and propagate reconstruction errors. This paper proposes an ICWE + SPMP + FWDC framework for simultaneous multi-target ISAR imaging. ICWE extracts fixed windows under multi-target masking. SPMP jointly estimates initial range cell locations and radial motion parameters through structured trajectory matching and search-space peeling. FWDC compensates the original mixed echo for each target and coherently combines the compensated components retained by their associated windows. The framework couples target-wise motion estimation with simultaneous focusing of overlapping targets. Experiments using ideal scattering center and electromagnetic simulation data demonstrate comparable visual focusing to the comparison methods under the evaluated low-SNR conditions with HRRP overlap, with less parameter estimation time and lower compensation cost. The results support a model-driven approach to simultaneous imaging without reconstructing individual target echoes.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Archaeological stratigraphy serves as the core foundation for establishing archaeological chronological sequences, clarifying ancient human activity patterns, and revealing the evolution of human–land relationships. Current field stratigraphic delineation mainly relies on manual visual observation and empirical judgement, which suffers from strong subjectivity and poor repeatability. It is difficult to accurately identify quasi-homogeneous archaeological strata with similar paedogenic backgrounds and soil characteristics, easily resulting in stratigraphic misclassification and boundary misjudgement. Hyperspectral imaging technology can effectively capture subtle spectral differences in soil physicochemical components and support the refined identification of homogeneous strata, whereas its practical application is restricted by high data dimensionality, severe band redundancy, and insufficient targeted extractions of effective features. Taking five typical archaeological deposits, including Warring States–Han, Song–Yuan, and Ming–Qing cultural layers, ash pits, and undisturbed subsoil, this study constructs a spectral–spatial collaborative deep learning model (ABS-Net) with an adaptive band selection module based on field-measured hyperspectral data of archaeological profiles. The proposed model realises the automatic mining of subtle spectral features and intelligent stratigraphic classification. Comparative experiments with multiple state-of-the-art hyperspectral classification models demonstrate that ABS-Net achieves competitive and stable classification accuracy. This study verifies the feasibility and application potential of combining hyperspectral remote sensing with deep learning for intelligent stratigraphic classification, providing a novel technical reference for intelligent stratigraphic interpretation in field archaeology.

</details>

Abstract (英文): The required amount of water for crop production differs from region to region, mainly due to the decreasing “effectiveness” of rainfall in maintaining plant growth due to increasing evaporation. Effective rainfall is related to the available moisture in the plant’s root zone, allowing the plant to germinate, emerge, and maintain its growth. There is, thus, a need to design agrometeorological and rain gauge networks for monitoring suitable crops, leading to sustainable agriculture. However, the minimum density needed for a rain-gauge network depends on the variability in space and time of the intended precipitation fields, and a variety of factors, including topographical features, accessibility, and ease of maintenance, influence the choice of rain-gauge locations. The number of rain gauges, rather than their location, is what optimizes a rain gauge network for various time scales, such as daily or monthly data. To map mean daily or mean monthly precipitation over watersheds or medium-sized agroclimatic zones and monitor crops for sustainable agriculture, this study focuses on the number of grid cells leading to the design of a rain gauge network, specifically a regular rain gauge grid scenario, considering the areal extent of land suitable for agriculture based on the agroclimatic zoning methodology. The universal kriging approach, an interpolation method based on geostatistical analysis, is used to calculate the required number of grid cells for the network’s density. Geostatistical analysis focuses on estimating and selecting the semi-variogram (SV) to determine the range or the decorrelation distance between rain gauges for precipitation estimation, superimposed on a grid mesh that encompasses the area’s agricultural land, such as Thessaly in Greece, as delineated by the agroclimatic zoning methodology. The daily and monthly mean climatological precipitation database is used, based on the CHIRPS-v3 dataset. The results indicate that the best fit for both daily and monthly mean precipitation is provided by the Spherical SV model, and the corresponding maximum range, which also represents the decorrelation distance, is calculated at 28.162 km for both mean daily precipitation and mean monthly precipitation, respectively. In summary, the size of the selected grid cells, based on the computed range of the selected SV, is 28 × 28 km2 for the region of Thessaly, where one rain gauge is expected to be installed in each grid, leading to a total number of 18 grid cells.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Geophysical and remote sensing surveys of Pacific Northwest Coast plankhouses, sweathouses, and other architectural features have been a growing component of Pacific Northwest Coast archaeological studies. Most of these studies have focused on coastal plankhouses with associated shell middens, especially in British Columbia. Limited geophysical examples exist of Pacific Northwest Coast architectural features from the southern region, including northern California and Oregon, especially in riverine settings. In this study, we integrate ground-penetrating radar and magnetic gradiometry to document subsurface features in collaboration with the Tsnungwe Council. Our findings, based on our interpretations of the observed data but not ground-truthed at this time, document subsurface features ranging from ~2 m wide pits to large rectangular plankhouses ~6 m wide and 12 m long. These houses include central subterranean pits, which contain a hearth, that are ~5 m wide. Our findings support the interpretation of a large multicomponent village site with several plankhouses present, providing a unique example of southern Pacific Northwest Coast Indigenous architecture. Future ground-truthing efforts may confirm and/or revise these interpretations.

Abstract (英文): Oriented remote sensing object detection (ORSOD) remains challenging because objects usually appear at arbitrary rotations, with large aspect-ratio variations and dense spatial layouts. Although recent detectors have achieved notable progress, most still rely on standard convolutional backbones, whose limited inherent rotation equivariance weakens robustness to rotated targets and reduces localization reliability. To address these issues, this paper proposes RedhDet, an ORSOD framework based on rotation-equivariant feature learning and dual-head decoupling. Specifically, a Ladder-Structured Implicitly Equivariant Network (LSIEN) is developed as the backbone to extract rotation-equivariant features in a soft manner, without relying on strictly constrained kernels or equivariant pre-trained weights. In addition, a decoupled dual-head architecture is introduced, in which a fully connected branch is used for classification and a convolutional branch is used for bounding box regression, thereby better matching the distinct requirements of semantic recognition and precise localization. Experimental results on the DOTA-1.0 and HRSC2016 datasets demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed method. On the DOTA-1.0 test set, RedhDet improves mAP50 by 2.09% and mAP75 by 14.84% over the baseline. On HRSC2016, gains of 14.12% and 51.85% are achieved for mAP50 and mAP75, respectively. With multi-scale training and testing, the proposed method achieves 80.40% mAP on DOTA-1.0, indicating its effectiveness and competitiveness for ORSOD.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Accurate and transferable flood segmentation from Earth observation (EO) imagery remains challenging because models trained on specific geographic regions and environmental conditions often exhibit limited generalization. This study proposes Global–Local Feature Context and Reconstruction (GL-FCR), a task-specific downstream decoder designed to adapt compact EO foundation-model representations for spatially detailed flood mapping. Integrated with Prithvi-EO-2.0, GL-FCR enhances the reconstruction of dense flood maps while maintaining contextual information from the pretrained representation. On the SEN1Floods11 standard test set, Prithvi + GL-FCR achieved a flood F1 score of 91.08%, compared with 90.02% on the geographically held-out Bolivia subset in the original experiment. Across three random seeds, GL-FCR achieved mean flood IoU scores of 83.07 ± 0.50% and 81.53 ± 0.27% on the standard test and Bolivia subsets, respectively, demonstrating reproducible performance across training runs. Additional SAR-only and optical–SAR experiments showed that TerraMind-B + GL-FCR achieved the strongest SAR-only performance, with F1 scores of 81.53% and 82.28% on the standard test and Bolivia subsets, respectively, whereas adding SAR to strong clear-scene optical representations provided limited additional benefit. Furthermore, zero-shot cross-dataset evaluation across all 509 WorldFloods v2 scenes yielded a flood F1 score of 84.56% and a flood IoU of 73.24% for Prithvi + GL-FCR, compared with 83.35% and 72.15%, respectively, for the Prithvi native decoder, without additional training or adaptation. Overall, the results demonstrate that task-specific global–local reconstruction can effectively leverage EO foundation-model representations for accurate and transferable flood segmentation across geographic regions and datasets.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Accurate prediction of significant wave height (SWH) is essential for ocean engineering safety, maritime transportation, and wave energy utilization. To address the nonlinear and non-stationary characteristics of SWH time series, this paper proposes a hybrid forecasting model that integrates signal decomposition, mode grouping and reconstruction, and deep learning. First, the model employs noise-assisted multivariate empirical mode decomposition (NA-MEMD) to adaptively decompose the multivariate time series into intrinsic mode functions (IMFs) to alleviate the problem of mode mixing. Subsequently, permutation entropy is employed to assess the complexity of the mode components and guide their grouping into two branches. Finally, WaveNet and long short-term memory (LSTM) networks are used to model and forecast the relatively high-complexity (fast-varying) and low-complexity (slow-varying) components, respectively, and the final forecast is generated by superimposing the predictions of all components. The proposed model was validated using measured data from two buoy stations, and evaluated by four metrics: root mean square error (RMSE), mean absolute error (MAE), symmetric mean absolute percentage error (sMAPE), and correlation coefficient (R). Experimental results show that the proposed model achieves lower RMSE, MAE, and sMAPE, and higher R compared with the baseline models across all evaluated forecast horizons and buoy sites.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Detecting submerged objects becomes difficult when suspended particles alter spectral signatures and reduce target–background contrast. A detector trained in clear water can consequently lose discrimination and produce extensive background responses as turbidity increases. Here, we present a target-specific framework that couples progressive domain adaptation with object-level response calibration. A nonlinear unmixing autoencoder first learns the target representation from labeled clear-water observations. Cross-turbidity progressive domain adaptation (CTP-DA) then transfers model states, target prototypes, and prediction memory through ordered unlabeled domains. Risk-gated object-level background suppression (RG-OBS) subsequently limits background activation while protecting supported target regions. Across the L1–L5 target domains of the controlled brick-and-stone benchmark, the framework achieved an average precision of 0.9282. This exceeded the strongest external comparator evaluated here by 7.10 percentage points. Ablation experiments linked the ranking improvement to progressive adaptation and the reduction in background response to subsequent spatial calibration. Together, these findings support combining intermediate unlabeled domains with spatial target evidence to sustain underwater detection across controlled turbidity changes.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Accurate spatial mapping of apple orchards is essential for regional agricultural planning and sustainable management; however, large-scale, multi-year mapping remains challenging because spectral confusion and climate-driven inter-annual phenological variation complicate interpretation. To address these challenges, we propose a Phenological Synchronization and Time–Frequency Feature Selection framework (FSITF) for high-precision apple orchard identification using Sentinel-2 time-series observations. We evaluated the framework in Shandong and Shaanxi provinces, two major apple-producing regions in China. FSITF consists of three main components: phenological synchronization, time–frequency feature construction, and discriminative feature selection. First, we develop a phenological synchronization strategy based on derivative analysis and correlation-based temporal alignment to reduce inter-annual and regional temporal discrepancies in phenological trajectories, thereby generating standardized cross-year phenological trajectories. Second, Harmonic Analysis is combined with temporal phenological metrics to construct a complementary time–frequency feature representation, enabling the characterization of complex perennial growth dynamics from both temporal evolution and periodic variation perspectives. Third, a dual-metric feature selection strategy integrating Jeffries–Matusita distance-based class separability and Random Forest-based feature importance is developed to identify informative features and reduce high-dimensional redundancy. Experimental results demonstrate that FSITF achieves overall accuracies exceeding 93% and Kappa coefficients above 0.85 in both study regions, outperforming conventional approaches without phenological synchronization and feature optimization. These results indicate that integrating phenological alignment, time–frequency feature representation, and orchard-oriented feature selection can effectively improve the accuracy and robustness of regional perennial crop mapping, providing a reliable framework for large-scale orchard monitoring.

</details>
